                                                                      DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS

                                                                                           GUO-NIU HAN


                                                  Abstract. For a sequence a = (a0 , a1 , . . . ) we define its dilated Hankel determinant Ḧn (a) =
                                                  det(a2i+j )0≤i,j≤n−1 , the minor of the infinite Hankel matrix (ai+j ) formed from the even-indexed
                                                  rows and the first n columns. We prove that, for a broad class of sequences, Ḧn admits a remark-
                                                  ably simple product evaluation. This mirrors the behaviour of the classical Hankel determinant
                                                  Hn , but with two key distinctions: the class of sequences for which such formulas are known is
                                                  far larger in the classical case; and, whereas Hn enjoys a single universal evaluation — the Heil-
                                                  ermann formula via the Jacobi continued fraction — no analogous general method exists for the
                                                  dilated determinant, which is therefore considerably more challenging. Our evaluations instead
                                                  rest on six methods developed here, four of general scope and two of a more specialised nature.
                                                  The cases treated include the factorial numbers, the Catalan and central binomial coefficients; the
                                                  Euler numbers and a one-parameter secant family; the involution numbers; the Springer numbers
                                                  along with elliptic and derivative deformations; the reciprocal-sine function, whose evaluation
                                                  rests on a new Catalan determinant proved by condensation; a Bessel analogue of the Euler num-
                                                  bers; and a multiplicative Bessel family. As an application, we settle a conjecture of Chapoton




arXiv:2607.08279v1 [math.CO] 9 Jul 2026
                                                  and the author on the roots of the Poupard and Kreweras polynomials.

                                        1. Introduction
                                                                                       n
                                                                              P
  Let a = (a0 , a1 , a2 , . . . ) be a sequence over a field, and let f (x) =  n≥0 an x /n! be its
exponential generating function. The (classical) Hankel determinant of order n is
                                                                a0    a1   a2   ···   an−1
                                                                a1    a2   a3   ···    an
                                                                a2    a3   a4   ···   an+1 .
                                           
           Hn = Hn (f ) = Hn (a) = det ai+j 0≤i,j≤n−1 =                                        (1.1)
                                                                 ..                     ..
                                                                  .                      .
                                                            an−1 an an+1 · · ·        a2n−2
Hankel determinants are a meeting point of orthogonal polynomials, the moment problem, con-
tinued fractions, total positivity and enumerative combinatorics. When the ak are the moments
of a linear functional µ, the determinants Hn are the Gram determinants of the monomial basis;
their positivity governs the classical moment problem and the total positivity of the Hankel ma-
trix [43, 39, 36], and the theorem of Heilermann and Stieltjes evaluates them through the Jacobi
continued fraction of the generating function [20, 47, 46, 12, 26, 27, 15]. Combinatorially, Hn
counts families of non-intersecting lattice paths [30, 13, 46, 34]; Flajolet’s theory [12] reads the
continued fraction off weighted Motzkin paths and gives, in one stroke, the continued-fraction
expansions — hence the Hankel determinants — of the Catalan, Bell, Stirling, Euler, tangent
and secant numbers, and of the elliptic functions. The Jacobi continued fraction, however, exists
only when all Hn ̸= 0; as soon as some Hn vanish it breaks down, and one turns instead to the
Hankel continued fraction [17, 16], which is defined for every sequence.
   A recurring theme of the subject is the contrast between sequences whose Hankel          determi-
nants have a closed product form — a formula of the NICE(n) type ξ Rat(n) i (ai n + bi )!±1
                                                                           n
                                                                                    Q
in Krattenthaler’s sense [27] — and those whose determinants carry sporadic large prime factors
and admit no such formula. The first class is recognised by its arithmetic, the prime factors
of Hn growing only linearly in n; it is this “niceness” that combinatorialists prize and that the
determinant calculus [26, 27] is designed to certify. Which sequences fall on which side is a subtle
matter, and it is this question that the present paper takes up — for a Hankel-type determinant
of a new kind.
Definition 1.1. The dilated Hankel determinant of order n of the sequence a = (a0 , a1 , . . . ) is
                                                          a0          a1   a2   ···   an−1
                                                          a2          a3   a4   ···   an+1
                                                          a4          a5   a6   ···   an+3 ,
                                          
      Ḧn = Ḧn (f ) = Ḧn (a) = det a2i+j 0≤i,j≤n−1 =                                         (1.2)
                                                           ..                           ..
                                                            .                            .
                                                         a2n−2 a2n−1 a2n · · ·        a3n−3

with the convention Ḧ0 = 1.
More generally, replacing the row index i by ri gives the r-step Hankel determinant
                                                
                                      det ari+j 0≤i,j≤n−1 ;
                                      DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                                 3

the classical Hankel determinant (1.1) is the case r = 1, and the dilated one the case r = 2.
This paper is devoted to r = 2. We call it the dilated Hankel determinant, or simply the dilated
determinant, throughout, and reserve the name 2-step for the rare occasions when it must be set
against the general r-step determinant (Section 3.1).
   Two readings of (1.2) place it within known theory. First, Ḧn is the minor of the infinite
Hankel matrix (ap+q )p,q≥0 that selects the even-indexed rows {0, 2, 4, . . . , 2n − 2} and the first n
columns; general minors of Hankel matrices fall under the determinant calculus of Krattenthaler
[26, 27]. Second, if ak = µ(z k ) are moments then a2i+j = µ(z 2i · z j ), so

                           Ḧn = det ⟨z 2i , z j ⟩µ 0≤i,j≤n−1 ,
                                                   
                                                                     ⟨p, q⟩µ := µ(pq),                        (1.3)

the Gram determinant of the bilinear form ⟨·, ·⟩µ on the two bases {1, z 2 , z 4 , . . . } and {1, z, z 2 , . . . }.
The factor 2 in a2i+j is thus the quadratic map z 7→ z 2 together with the even/odd splitting of
µ — the realm of the quadratic decomposition and symmetrisation of orthogonal polynomials
[5] and of biorthogonal and multiple orthogonal polynomials [32, 21]. The closely related but
distinct shifted Hankel determinants det(ai+j+k ) of [17] translate the index by a constant rather
than scaling it; the interaction of the two operations — shifts of a dilated determinant — is itself
a source of clean evaluations (Sections 8, 14, 22 and 24).

Results. Our central finding is that, for a wide range of classical sequences, Ḧn has a surprisingly
simple product formula even when the ordinary Hn does not. We organise the evaluations into a
few families.
      • The Beta family am+1 /am = ρ (m + α)/(m + β) (Section 4) — the sequences whose term
        ratio has degree less than or equal to 1 — has a single closed form (Theorem 4.1) spe-
        cialising to the factorials, the Catalan numbers, the central binomial coefficients and the
        double factorials. The resulting dilated Hankel determinants, such as the Catalan val-
        ues 1, 3, 32, 1232, 172032, . . . and the central binomial values 1, 8, 224, 22528, 8200192, . . . ,
        appear not to have been recorded before.
                                                                                              n Q
      • The involution numbers and the Gaussian family exp(cx+bx2 ) give Ḧn = (4bc)( 2 ) k<n k!
        (Theorem 5.1, Section 5); the Gaussian is the unique exponential weight for which this
        simplification occurs.                          Rx
      • The Euler number family 1/ cos(x)s+1 + 0 dy/ cos(y)t+1 (Proposition 7.1, Section 7),
        whose (s, t) = (0, 1) member has the Euler (secant–tangent) numbers [2] as its moments,
        has a closed product evaluation for every admissible (s, t), even though the relevant odd
        moment functional is not classical.
      • The secant-number family (1+x)/ cos(x)s+1 , a separate family, has the product evaluation
        Ḧn = cn n−1
                   Q
                     i=1 (s + 1)i valid for every s (Theorem 12.1, Section 12), even though its odd
        moment functional is not classical once s ≥ 1.
      • The Springer number family 1/(cos x − t sin x)r [40, 39], whose t = 1, r = 1 member has
        the Springer numbers as its moments, a derivative (cos x + sin x)/(cos x − sin x)s of it, and
        an elliptic Euler family all have product formulas (Theorems 16.1, 17.1, 19.1).
      • An algebraic family (1 + x)/(1 − x2 )s/2 (Theorem 20.1, Section 20) also has a product
        formula for every s, even though neither of its two moment functionals is classical; the
        proof accordingly abandons orthogonal polynomials altogether in favour of an elementary
        contiguous relation in s.
      • A rank-one perturbation (sin x + 1)/ cos2 x + s sin x of the Euler number family (Propo-
        sition 15.1, Section 15) factors for every s, with Ḧn affine in s; at the member s = −1,
        where it becomes (1 + sin3 x)/          2
                                            cos x, the seemingly sporadic prime factors 29, 37, 23 at
                                         n
        n = 8, 9, 10 turn out to be 2 + 1.
      • The reciprocal-sine function (1 + x) x/ sin x (Theorem 18.1, Section 18) is evaluated as
        a product of factorials — the deepest evaluation of the paper. Its two moment func-
        tionals turn out to be Wilson functionals differing in a single parameter; the connection
        coefficients of the biorthogonal reduction then collapse to a single hypergeometric term,
4                                             GUO-NIU HAN

       and the evaluation reduces to a determinant of products of two Catalan numbers, estab-
       lished by Desnanot–Jacobi condensation (Theorem 18.8) — a determinant evaluation of
       independent interest.             Rx
     • A Bessel (s, t) family cosbs (x) + 0 cosbt (y) dy (Theorem 23.1, Section 23), built from the
       normalised Bessel function cosbs = Γ(s + 1)(2/x)s Js (x), has a closed product evaluation
       for every (s, t): the signed factor of the Euler number family reappears at doubled argu-
       ment, so the determinants are nonzero exactly on the half-integer offsets t − s ∈ 12 + Z
       and vanish eventually on the integer ones — the Bessel ladder walks in half steps.
     • A multiplicative Bessel family (1 + x) cosbν2 (Theorem 25.4, Section 25), the Bessel ana-
       logue of the secant-number family with the exponent ladder replaced by the order ladder,
       has a closed product evaluation at every even order n = 2N , complete with quarter-integer
       zeros — the blocks (2ν + 12 )k — that have no trigonometric counterpart. Although its even
       moment functional is not classical, Watson’s product formula makes the even moments
       a single hypergeometric term, and the evaluation falls, unexpectedly, to the elementary
       Vandermonde reduction, whose only other application is the Beta family; at odd orders
       the degree count fails by one and an irreducible carrier appears.

Origin and application. As is so often the way with determinant evaluations [26], the object of
this paper first announced itself in the course of another problem. While studying a conjecture of
Chapoton and the author on the roots of the Poupard and Kreweras polynomials [4], the author
was confronted with a determinant that did not yield to the ordinary continued-fraction machinery
and whose first row was the secant-type sequence 1, 1, 1, 3, 5, 25, 61, 427, . . . of (1 + x)/ cos x; this
clue is what led to the dilated determinant studied here. We close the circle in Section 26:
Conjecture 5.4 of [4] is, after a change of basis and up to an explicit power of two, the dilated
Hankel determinant of (1 + x)/ cos x (Theorem 26.3), whose evaluation (Proposition 11.1) settles
it.
Methods. The classical Hankel determinant enjoys a universal method of evaluation: the Jacobi
continued fraction of the generating function produces the whole sequence H1 , H2 , . . . at once,
from a single list of coefficients [20, 43, 47]. For the dilated determinant there is no such tool. Ḧn
is in general a substantially harder object: no one method evaluates every family. We therefore
develop six methods for Ḧn , collected and abstracted in Section 3 and tagged M1–M6 throughout
the paper. Four of them run through the paper: the elementary Vandermonde (row-factorisation)
reduction M1; the biorthogonal reduction M2, which splits a into its even and odd moment
functionals — the determinantal face of the even/odd contraction of continued fractions [47, 12]
— and reduces Ḧn to a determinant of connection coefficients; the one-functional reduction M3;
and the divisor method M4, which evaluates a one-parameter family by its degenerations through
a dilated Cauchy–Binet expansion. Each of these four comes with a sharp rigidity boundary
marking where the product form must fail. The remaining two methods are of a different, more
specialised character, each used only once rather than running through the paper: the algebraic
family (Section 20) is settled by a contiguous relation (M5), and the rank-one perturbation
(Section 15) by the matrix-determinant lemma (M6). Finally, the reciprocal-sine function of
Section 18 is reached by M2 followed by a new finishing step: the connection determinant turns
out to be a determinant of products of Catalan numbers, which we evaluate by Desnanot–Jacobi
condensation; the same finishing step closes the Bessel (s, t) family of Section 23, whose connection
determinant carries the parameters in its entries. In the opposite direction, the multiplicative
Bessel family of Section 25 is closed by M1 alone — its even moments are a single hypergeometric
term, and at even orders the row factorisation lands exactly on the Vandermonde degree bound:
the one evaluation of the paper where the most elementary method reaches beyond the Beta
family. The classical background needed — orthogonal polynomials, the moment problem, J- and
S-fractions and their contractions — is gathered, in self-contained algebraic form, in Section 2.
Organisation. Section 2 fixes notation and recalls the classical toolbox; Section 3 presents the
six methods M1–M6 and closes with a table matching them to families. The evaluations then
proceed family by family: the Beta family (Section 4); the Gaussian family — containing the
involution numbers — and its derivative rule (Sections 5 and 6); the Euler number family with its
                                 DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                        5

double and single shifts (Sections 7–10); the secant-number family (1+x)/ cos(x)s+1 with its shifts
(Sections 11–14); the rank-one perturbation of the Euler number family (Section 15); the Springer
number family and a derivative of it (Sections 16–17); the reciprocal-sine case (Section 18); the
elliptic Euler family (Section 19); the algebraic and squared algebraic families with their shifted
determinants (Sections 20–22); the Bessel (s, t) family with its double shift (Sections 23 and 24);
and the multiplicative Bessel family at even orders (Section 25). Section 26 settles the Chapoton–
Han conjecture, and Section 27 collects, family by family, the explicit corollaries and their shifts.
Section 28 then sketches the Lindström–Gessel–Viennot point of view — a natural method which,
for the dilated determinant, yields no evaluation and which no proof of this paper uses; it is
recorded for orientation only. Section 29 records the classical Hankel determinants Hn of several
of the moment sequences studied here. Finally Section 30 gathers the concluding remarks and
open problems. All closed forms were discovered and checked with SageMath.

                                        2. Preliminaries
   This section collects the classical material we use, in the self-contained algebraic form suited
to formal power series; no analysis or positivity is needed. Standard references are [44, 5, 21]
for orthogonal polynomials, [47, 12, 46, 26] for continued fractions and Hankel determinants. A
reader fluent in this material may skip to the methods of Section 3 and refer back as needed.
Conventions. Throughout the paper, m!! = m(m − 2)(m − 4) · · · , down to 1 or 2, is the double
factorial, with 0!! = (−1)!! = 1; empty products equal 1; (α)m = α(α + 1) · · · (α + m − 1) is the
Pochhammer symbol; and αd = 0 for P      d < 0. For sequences given by a generating function we
use the exponential convention f (x) = n≥0 an xn /n!, i.e. an = n! [xn ]f .

2.1. Desnanot–Jacobi condensation. Several of the evaluations below are closed by induction
on the order, through a classical identity among the minors of a matrix. For an n × n matrix M
                                                                                                i,i′
write M ji for the (n − 1) × (n − 1) minor obtained by deleting row i and column j, and M j,j      ′

for the (n − 2) × (n − 2) minor obtained by deleting rows i, i′ and columns j, j ′ . The Desnanot–
Jacobi identity — equivalently the Jacobi–Desnanot identity, and, after Lewis Carroll, Dodgson
condensation [26] — states, for n ≥ 2,
                                 1,n
                     det M det M 1,n = det M 11 det M nn − det M n1 det M 1n :                  (2.1)
the full determinant times its central minor equals a difference of products of the four minors
obtained by deleting one boundary row and one boundary column. When a determinant belongs
to a family stable under (2.1) — so that all five minors are again members of the family — the
identity becomes a two-step recurrence in the order, and matching it against a candidate product
proves a closed form; this is the finishing step used in Sections 18 and 23.

2.2. Moments, the Hankel determinant, and orthogonal polynomials. A linear func-
tional L on the polynomial ring Q[y] (more generally over any field) is fixed by its values on the
monomial basis,
                                   µk := L[y k ]   (k ≥ 0),
the moments of L. The Hankel determinant of the moment sequence is
              Hn := det µi+j 0≤i,j≤n−1 = det L[y i · y j ] 0≤i,j≤n−1 ,
                                                         
                                                                       H0 = 1,

i.e. the Gram determinant of 1, y, . . . , y n−1 under the symmetric bilinear form ⟨p, q⟩ := L[pq].
We assume L is quasi-definite, Hn ̸= 0 for all n; this is exactly the condition under which the
Gram–Schmidt process never breaks down.
   Under this hypothesis there is a unique sequence of monic orthogonal polynomials P0 =
1, P1 , P2 , . . . with deg Pn = n and
                             L[Pn Pm ] = hn δnm ,     hn := L[Pn2 ] ̸= 0,
where hn is the squared norm of Pn (a formal one — no positivity is assumed, so hn need not be
a square; we abbreviate it to norm throughout). They are the Gram–Schmidt orthogonalisation

2.3. Change of family. Every reduction in this paper replaces the row and column families
of a moment matrix by monic families of the same degrees. That this leaves the determinant
unchanged is the following elementary but pivotal fact, which we isolate once and cite throughout.
Lemma 2.1 (change of family). Let L be a linear functional on a space of polynomials, and
let (Ri )0≤i<n and (Cj )0≤j<n be two families of polynomials. If (R̃i ) and (C̃j ) are unitriangular
recombinations,                      X                         X
                          R̃i = Ri +    pik Rk ,    C̃j = Cj +     qjl Cl ,
                                           k<i                                 l<j
then                                                             
                           det L[R̃i C̃j ] 0≤i,j<n = det L[Ri Cj ] 0≤i,j<n .
In particular, if the matrix L[R̃i C̃j ] is triangular, then det L[Ri Cj ] = n−1
                                                                          Q
                                                                              i=0 L[R̃i C̃i ].

In every application the new families are monic of strictly increasing degree—typically the orthog-
onal polynomials of the relevant functional—hence unitriangular recombinations of the monomial
(or split-monomial) families they replace.
2.4. The three-term recurrence and Favard’s theorem. Because yPn has degree n + 1,
expansion (2.3) writes it in the basis P0 , . . . , Pn+1 ,

Conversely, Favard’s theorem states that any monic sequence defined by a recurrence of the shape
(2.4) with all λn ̸= 0 is orthogonal for a (unique up to scale) quasi-definite functional. So the
data (cn , λn ) and the moment data (µk ) determine each other.

2.5. Continued fractions: the J-fraction and S-fraction. The translation between moments
and recurrence coefficients is most compact as a continued fraction. The Jacobi continued fraction
(J-fraction) of L is
                                     X                         µ0
                                         µk x k =                                  ,                (2.6)
                                                                    λ1 x2
                                     k≥0          1 − c0 x −

                                                                          1 − ..
where cn , λn are exactly the recurrence coefficients of (2.4); this is the theorem of Stieltjes [47, 12],
and with (2.5) it is the standard route to closed-form Hankel determinants [20]. Combinatorially
µn is the generating polynomial of weighted Motzkin paths of length n, a level step at height k
weighted ck and a fall from height k (paired with the matching rise) weighted λk [12, 46].
   When µ0 = 1, the series often admits the narrower Stieltjes continued fraction (S-fraction):

with coefficients b1 , b2 , . . . ; here µn counts weighted Dyck-type paths.

2.6. The even contraction S → J. The S-fraction (2.7) and the J-fraction (2.6) are different
continued fractions — one has only first-order terms bj x, the other both cn x and λn x2 — yet they
can expand the same power series. Indeed a series given by an S-fraction also has a J-fraction: the
even contraction of (2.7) is, by definition, the continued fraction whose sequence of convergents
is the even-indexed subsequence C0 , C2 , C4 , . . . of the convergents C0 , C1 , C2 , . . . of (2.7); it turns
out to be a J-fraction. The bj are therefore not themselves recurrence coefficients, but determine
the cn , λn through this contraction, which yields

2.7. The odd contraction. The odd contraction of (2.7) is, by definition, the continued fraction

                  3. Six methods for the dilated Hankel determinant
   Six methods for Ḧn are developed in this paper. Four of them run through it, listed here
from the most elementary to the most structured; each evaluates the dilated determinant under
a different structural hypothesis on a, and each has a boundary beyond which the product form
fails; Subsection 3.5 collects, in tabular form, the hypotheses, the collapse conditions and the
applications of all the methods.
   M1. The Vandermonde reduction (Section 3.1) factors each row and uses no functional at all; it
         applies to the sequences whose term ratio has degree ≤ 1 (the Beta family) and, through
         the dilation, to the even orders of the multiplicative Bessel family (Section 25), whose
         even moments have a hypergeometric term ratio of degree (2, 2).
   M2. The biorthogonal reduction (Section 3.2) splits a into its even and odd functionals S, T
         and reduces Ḧn to a determinant of connection coefficients; it is exact whenever both are
         quasi-definite, and collapses to a product when the connection coefficients are themselves
         explicit — notably when S and T belong to a single classical family and differ in one
         parameter, so that the connection array is a single hypergeometric term (see The collapse
         mechanism at the end of Section 3.2).
   M3. The one-functional reduction (Section 3.3), the mechanism of Cigler and Krattenthaler
         [6, 29], uses the orthogonal polynomials of the single functional E on z; the Gaussian is
         the only Appell family for which it collapses.
   M4. The divisor method, with the dilated Cauchy–Binet lemma (Section 3.4), treats Ḧn (fθ )
         as a polynomial in a parameter and locates its zeros at the degenerations of fθ .
Methods M1 and M4 are elementary; M2 and M3 rest on the orthogonal-polynomial prelimi-
naries of Section 2. We tag results by the method used throughout the paper.
   The remaining two methods are of a more specialised character, each used only once rather
than running through the paper, and are tagged accordingly.
   M5. The contiguous-relation method (Section 20) applies when neither moment functional is
         classical: it relates Ḧn at a parameter s to Ḧn at s−2 by an elementary column operation,
         solves the resulting relation by a periodicity argument, and fixes the constant at a single
         special value. No orthogonal polynomials are used.
   M6. The rank-one/matrix-determinant-lemma method (Section 15, Remark 3.12) applies to a
         rank-one perturbation of a family already evaluated by another method: the perturbed
                                  DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                          9

        determinant is the unperturbed one times an explicit scalar, computed by the matrix-
        determinant lemma rather than by the divisor method M4.
3.1. Row factorisation and the Vandermonde reduction (M1). The most elementary re-
duction uses no functional, no positivity and no continued fraction. It evaluates the generalised
Hankel determinant det(axi +j ) with arbitrary row indices x0 , x1 , . . . , xn−1 — the dilated deter-
minant Ḧn being the case xi = 2i, and the r-step Hankel determinant det(ari+j ) the case xi = ri
— whenever the rows of the matrix separate into a row-dependent prefactor times a polynomial
in the row index.
Lemma 3.1 (Vandermonde reduction). Let (am ) be a sequence over a field K, let n ≥ 1, and
suppose there are a scalar function x 7→ w(x) and polynomials Q0 , . . . , Qn−1 ∈ K[x] with deg Qj ≤

   The reduction (3.2) is classical determinant calculus: it is the mechanism behind Kratten-
thaler’s determinant lemma [24, Lemma 2.2], [26]. What the present section adds is the delim-
itation of its exact range — the term-ratio boundary of degree at most one (3.3) below — and
the even-index escape that reaches the multiplicative Bessel family of Section 25.
   The factorisation (3.1) is available precisely for the sequences whose term ratio is rational of
degree at most one. Indeed, if am+1 /am = R(m) is rational with numerator and denominator of
degrees p and q, then ax+j /ax = j−1
                                     Q
                                       l=0 R(x + l); clearing the common denominator over the shifts
l = 0, . . . , n − 2 leaves ax+j = w(x) Qj (x) with deg Qj = p j + q (n − 1 − j), which is ≤ n − 1 for
all j < n iff p ≤ 1 and q ≤ 1, i.e.
                                            am+1       m+α
                                                  =ρ          .                                  (3.3)

This is the three-parameter Beta family: here w(x) = ax /(x + β)n−1 and Qj (x) = ρj (x + α)j (x +
β + j)n−1−j , and Lemma 3.1 yields the closed form, computed in Section 4, of which the factorial,
Catalan and central binomial determinants are specialisations; the triangular case deg Qj = j is
the degeneration q = 0 (denominator absent). Conversely, a term ratio of degree ≥ 2 inflates some
deg Qj beyond n − 1, the collapse onto the Vandermonde determinant fails, and the determinants
acquire large sporadic prime factors (Section 4). One escape from this boundary exists and is
used once: in the dilated determinant only even row indices occur, so (3.1) is needed only in the
contracted row variable i (with xi = i), where a hypergeometric term ratio of degree (2, 2) on the
even-indexed half of a still produces polynomials of admissible degree — fitting the bound n − 1
exactly when n is even; this closes the multiplicative Bessel family of Section 25.
3.2. The biorthogonal reduction (M2). Let a = (an )n≥0 be a sequence over a field, split
into its even and odd parts. We reduce Ḧn = det(a2i+j )0≤i,j≤n−1 to a determinant of connection
coefficients (Lemma 3.4) under the sole hypothesis that its even and odd parts are quasi-definite
moment sequences; no continued-fraction assumption is needed at this stage, and the closed-form
evaluation follows once the data are specialised (Proposition 7.1). Throughout write n̄ = ⌈n/2⌉
and n = ⌊n/2⌋, so n̄ + n = n and n̄ ∈ {n, n + 1}.

The collapse mechanism. Lemma 3.4 is exact, but it leaves the determinant of the connection
array to be evaluated, and for a generic pair of classical functionals this array is a balanced 3 F2 -
type double sum, out of reach of the determinant calculus. In every case where M2 closes in this
paper, one and the same two-stage mechanism is at work. First, S and T lie in a single classical
family and differ in a single parameter; for such a pair the connection coefficients collapse from
a double sum to one hypergeometric term, which splits into a row factor, a column factor, and a
kernel depending only on i − m and (possibly) i + m. Second, the resulting kernel determinant
— which carries the shift n̄ of Lemma 3.4, hence free integer parameters — is evaluated in
closed form. The paper contains three instances. For the Euler (s, t) family the two functionals
differ in the exponent parameter, the kernel is the binomial coefficient (t−s)/2
                                                                                  
                                                                             i−m , and the shifted
binomial determinant is evaluated by the dual Jacobi–Trudi identity (Proposition 7.1). For
the reciprocal-sine function the two functionals are Wilson functionals differing in one Wilson
parameter, the kernel is the product of Catalan numbers Ci−m−1 Ci+m , and the shifted Catalan
determinant is evaluated by Desnanot–Jacobi condensation (Theorem 18.8). For the Bessel (s, t)
family the two   functionals are Jacobi–Gegenbauer   functionals differing in one parameter, the
            t−s
                                                  
kernel is i−m       (t + 2m + 1)i−m (s + i + m)i−m — binomial in i − m but carrying the parameters
through i + m — and the shifted connection determinant is again evaluated by condensation
(Theorem 23.7). When the two classical functionals do not form such a one-parameter pair, the
array remains a genuine double sum and no product formula is to be expected.

3.3. A one-functional reduction (M3). The biorthogonal reduction of Section 3.2 splits E into
its even and odd parts S, T and passes to the contracted variable y = z 2 . A parallel reduction
keeps the single functional E[z k ] = ak on z and uses its own orthogonal polynomials. It needs E
itself to be quasi-definite—a hypothesis independent of the quasi-definiteness of S and T —and
it underlies the Hermite evaluation of the Gaussian family in Section 5.
   Assume E is quasi-definite, with monic orthogonal polynomials (πm )m≥0 . By (1.2), Ḧn =
det E[z 2i · z j ] 0≤i,j≤n−1 .
   The reduction collapses to a product as soon as, alongside the columns, the rows too can be
replaced by a one-sidedly orthogonal family. Suppose there are monic polynomials Φi of degree
2i, each a unitriangular recombination of the even monomials 1, z 2 , . . . , z 2i , orthogonal to every
polynomial of degree < i:
                                       E[Φi πj ] = 0        (j < i).
Such a Φi exists and is unique whenever the corresponding Gram minor is nonzero.

Lemma 3.6 (one-functional reduction). If the even-orthogonal family (Φi )i<n above exists, then
                                                  n−1
                                                  Y
                                          Ḧn =         E[Φi πi ].                               (3.11)
                                                  i=0

Proof. Replace the column family (z j )j<n by the orthogonal family (πj )j<n and the row family
(z 2i )i<n by (Φi )i<n ; both are unitriangular recombinations, so Lemma 2.1 gives
                                                           
                                        Ḧn = det E[Φi πj ] 0≤i,j<n .

By orthogonality E[Φi πj ] = 0 for j < i, so this matrix is upper triangular and its determinant is
the product of the diagonal entries E[Φi πi ].                                                   □

   Equation (3.11) is the one-functional counterpart of Lemma 3.4. Section 5 carries it out for
the Gaussian functional, whose orthogonal polynomials are the Hermite polynomials and whose
Φi has a closed umbral form. The passage from a Hankel determinant to a triangular product
built from the orthogonal polynomials of a single functional is precisely the mechanism developed
by Cigler and Krattenthaler [6, 29], who reduce the Hankel determinant of a linear combination
of moments of orthogonal polynomials to a smaller determinant of those polynomials and, as we
do throughout, close several of their evaluations by Desnanot–Jacobi (Dodgson) condensation.
                                    DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                          13

3.4. The divisor method and the dilated Cauchy–Binet lemma (M4). The reductions
M1–M3 evaluate Ḧn from the moment data directly. The divisor method (M4) instead applies to
a family fθ depending on a parameter θ: one treats Ḧn (fθ ) as a polynomial in θ, bounds its degree,
and locates its zeros at the degenerate values of θ where fθ collapses to a geometric/exponential
function or to an even function. At such a value the moments form an exponential sum, and the
dilated determinant is evaluated, to leading order, by a single Cauchy–Binet expansion into two
Vandermonde determinants. Three general facts drive the method; the case-specific inputs (the
degree bound and the precise orders) are supplied in each application (Sections 16, 17 and 19).
   The first is a homogeneity, used to rescale and to take limits.
                                                                   n
Lemma 3.7 (homogeneity). For scalars β, c, Ḧn f (βx) = β 3( 2 ) Ḧn (f ) and Ḧn c f = c n Ḧn (f ).

factor c multiplies every entry, hence the determinant by cn .                                          □

  The second is the bookkeeping principle that turns “enough vanishing” into an exact product.
Observation 3.8 (divisor principle). Let D(θ) be a polynomialPof degree ≤ N , not  P identically zero,
that vanishes to order ≥ dk at distinct points θ1 , . . . , θs . If k dk ≥ N , then k dk = N , every
order is exact, and D(θ) = c sk=1 (θ − θk )dk with c ̸= 0 fixed by the value of D at any further
                              Q
point.
  The third, and the engine of the method, expands a dilated determinant whose moments are
an exponential sum.

   The first determinant is a Vandermonde in the squared nodes ζν2 , and this squaring is the
algebraic imprint of the dilation. Indeed the even rows enter only through ζν2i = (ζν2 )i , which
depends on ζν only through ζν2 ; hence these rows cannot tell a node ζν apart from its negative
−ζν (the two share the same square). This is exactly why, in the symmetric case above, a node
and its negative collapse the term.
   We use Lemma 3.9 in the next sections in the following way. When the exponential sum
depends on a parameter and the nodes and coefficients degenerate as the parameter tends to a
special value, the same expansion lets us read off the order to which Ḧn vanishes there, and its
leading coefficient. Suppose then that the exponential sum of Lemma 3.9 depends on a parameter
ϵ,

Proof. (i)PEvery surviving term of (3.13) has order ≥ ν∈T oν ≥ M : the coefficient product has
order ≥ ν∈T oν , and the two determinants, being polynomials in the ζν (ϵ), have order ≥ 0.
Hence so has the sum.
   (ii) By (i) it remains to read off the coefficient of ϵM . Since T ∗ is the unique minimiser, every
other surviving term is O(ϵM +1 ), so only T ∗ contributes; taking the leading part c̄ν ϵ oν of each
coefficient and setting ϵ = 0 in the two determinants gives C. It is nonzero: the first determinant
is a Vandermonde in the pairwise-distinct ζ̄ν2 , hence the ζ̄ν are themselvesQ    pairwise distinct and
the second determinant, a Vandermonde in the ζ̄ν , is nonzero as well; and ν∈T ∗ c̄ν ̸= 0 because
the orders on T ∗ are exact.                                                                          □
   The corollary above governs the distinct-node case, where the limiting nodes ζ̄ν stay separate.
The opposite extreme is confluent: the nodes merge to a single point, and a Vandermonde degen-
erates into a Wronskian — the determinant of a column together with its successive derivatives.
This is what happens when an even generating function is translated by a parameter ϕ, that
is gϕ (x) = g(x + ϕ): as the next lemma shows, each column of the dilated matrix of gϕ is the
ϕ-derivative of the one before it, so Ḧn (gϕ ) is literally a Wronskian in ϕ.

                                        4. The Beta family
   Throughout this section a = (am )m≥0 is a plain sequence: no exponential normalisation is
applied, and the entries of the determinants are the am themselves. We assume that the term
ratio am+1 /am is a rational function of m with numerator and denominator of degree at most
one — the exact hypothesis under which the Vandermonde reduction M1 of Section 3.1 operates.
The generic case, numerator and denominator both of degree one, is the Beta family proper
(Theorem 4.1); the degenerate case with denominator of degree zero — β absent — is treated
separately (Proposition 4.2). Such sequences form the three-parameter family
                                           (α)m         am+1    m+α
                             am = a0 ρm         ,            =ρ     .                                  (4.1)
                                           (β)m          am     m+β
   The name is explained by the case β > α > 0, a0 = ρ = 1: there (4.1) is the moment sequence
                                                                                     R1
of the Beta distribution on [0, 1] with density xα−1 (1 − x)β−α−1 /B(α, β − α), since 0 xm dµ =
(α)m /(β)m . Beta distributions are the model solutions of the Hausdorff moment problem on a
bounded interval, the polynomials orthogonal to them are the Jacobi polynomials [5, 21], and the
16                                             GUO-NIU HAN

associated Jacobi continued fraction [47, 12] governs the classical (one-step) Hankel determinants
of the family. None of this positivity is needed below: the scale ρ and the total mass a0 are
inessential, and α, β, ρ may be taken as arbitrary parameters, the determinant identities being
polynomial (or rational) in them. Members of (4.1) include the   factorials    (m + r)! = r! (r + 1)m
                                                         1   2m        m ( 1 ) /(2) , and the central
(degenerate, β absent), the Catalan numbers Cm = m+1         m    =  4     2 m     m
binomial coefficients bm = 2m        m ( 1 ) /(1) .

   The section is organised as follows. Subsection 4.1 proves a single closed form for det(axi +j )
with arbitrary row indices xi (Theorem 4.1); Subsection 4.2 specialises it to the dilated, shifted
and r-step determinants of the classical members; Subsection 4.3 records where the mechanism
stops.

xi = ri + t evaluates every shifted r-step determinant det(ari+j+t ) of the family in closed form.

4.3. The boundary of the mechanism.

Remark 4.10. Theorem 4.1 also delimits the mechanism. If the term ratio am+1 /am isa ratio-
                                                                    1   3m            3m
nal function of degree d ≥ 2 — as for the Fuss–Catalan numbers 2m+1      m or for m — the
polynomials Qj acquire degree d(n − 1) > n − 1 and the collapse onto the Vandermonde deter-
minant fails; and indeed the corresponding dilated Hankel determinants exhibit large sporadic
prime factors. The same holds for sequences that are not of hypergeometric type at all (Motzkin,
Delannoy, Apéry, Franel, Bell, . . . ).

Remark 4.11. The formulas of this section explain the experimental observation that these de-
terminants factorise into very small primes: a ratio of factorials of arguments at most 4n has no
prime factor beyond 4n.


                            5. Involutions and the Gaussian family
   The Hankel determinants of the Gaussian (normal) moment sequence — and, more generally,
of moment sequences of orthogonal polynomial families such as the Hermite polynomials — are
classical, and are treated in the standard references on the moment problem and orthogonal
polynomials: Shohat and Tamarkin [38], Akhiezer [1] and Szegő [44]. The evaluation below is a
dilated (row-shifted) refinement of that classical Hankel determinant.
   The Beta family of Section 4 is a plain sequence, and its evaluation was the Vandermonde
reductionP M1. In this section the sequence is given by an exponential generating function,
f (x) = n an xn /n!, and the tool is the one-functional reduction M3 (Lemma 3.6). We carry
it out on its simplest instance, the Gaussian family: a single orthogonal family (the Hermite
polynomials) suffices, and the dilated Hankel determinant is triangularised directly.
   The bookkeeping principle behind every evaluation here is Lemma 2.1, the change-of-family
lemma: replacing the row and column families of a moment matrix by monic families of the
same degrees leaves the determinant unchanged, and the triangular outcome is then read off the
diagonal. Here the new families are the Hermite polynomials Hej and the even family Φi of
Lemma 5.4, both monic of strictly increasing degree, hence unitriangular recombinations of the
(split-)monomial families they replace.

Theorem 5.1. Let c be an indeterminate (or any scalar) and let (an ) be defined by
                 X xn                                  Xn

Remark 5.5 (where the definition comes from). The formula for Φi is not guessed; it is forced. We

Remark 5.7 (Gaussian rigidity among Appell   families). The evaluation hinges on the shift identity
                                           p p−q
of Lemma 5.3, L[Hep (c + z) Heq (z)] = q! q c , which expresses that the Hermite polynomials
                                            m                tz with G(t) = e−t2 /2 . The method
                            P
form an Appell sequence,      m≥0 Hem (z) t /m! = G(t) e
therefore asks for a family (πm ) that is at once the sequence of monic orthogonal polynomials
of L and an Appell sequence, and these two requirements already pin down the weight. Indeed,
normalising G(0) = 1 and L[1] = 1, orthogonality gives L[eαz ] = 1/G(α) and makes the mixed
generating function
                              X                     tp sq   G(t) G(s)
                                   L[πp (z) πq (z)]       =
                                                    p! q!   G(t + s)
                                p,q≥0
a function of ts alone. With γ = log G this reads γ(t) + γ(s) − γ(t + s) = ψ(ts), and matching
the coefficient of ta sb for a ̸= b, a, b ≥ 1, forces γk = 0 for k ≥ 3, i.e. γ(t) = γ1 t + γ2 t2 .
Hence L is Gaussian: up to a translation of z (the term γ1 ) and a scaling (Remark 5.6(i)), the
Hermite/Gaussian case is the only Appell family to which the one-functional triangularisation
applies.

                         6. The single shift of the Gaussian family
   The same Hermite machinery (again the one-functional reduction M3) yields the derivative
rule for this family. Since differentiation shifts the coefficient sequence, an (f ′ ) = an+1 (f ), the
dilated Hankel determinant of f ′ is the shifted dilated Hankel determinant of f : Ḧn (f ′ ) =
det(a2i+j+1 )0≤i,j≤n−1 . (For the rational chain the corresponding rule is the ratio formula in
Corollary 4.4.)

                                          7. The Euler number family
  The Euler numbers En are defined by [2, 43, 45, 18]
                           X    xn
           tan x + sec x =   En ,       (En )n≥0 = 1, 1, 1, 2, 5, 16, 61, 272, 1385, . . .          (7.1)

                                    n≥0

The even Euler numbers E2n (resp. odd E2n+1 ) are the secant (resp. tangent) numbers; by André’s
theorem En is the number of alternating permutations of {1, . . . , n}. The ordinary generating
functions of the secant and tangent numbers have the classical Stieltjes S-fractions

above.

   The proof occupies Subsections 7.1–7.2 and is an instance of the collapse mechanism of Sec-

is specialised in Section 8 and in the corollaries of Section 27.

                      8. The double shift of the Euler number family
  Alongside Ḧn = det(a2i+j ) the most regular variant is the double shift

                                 Ḧn(2) := det a2i+j+2 0≤i,j≤n−1 .
                                                      
                                                                                                      (8.1)

Shifting the column index by 2 preserves parity — in the moment picture (3.4) the even columns
stay S-columns and the odd columns stay T -columns, each merely advanced by one power of y
— so no even/odd swap occurs and the determinant stays a product for all (s, t). (The single
shift det(a2i+j+1 ), which swaps the two parities and is far less regular, is taken up in Section 9.)
   Since a2i+j+2 = E[y i+1 χj ], the double shift is the dilated determinant of the Christoffel trans-
form y E of E by y = z 2 : its even and odd parts are S ′ [p] := S[yp] and T ′ [p] := T [yp]; the
biorthogonal reduction M2 again applies. We first record what this transform does to the in-
                                                              (2)
gredients of Section 7 for arbitrary uj , vj , and express Ḧn through the transformed connection
determinant; only afterwards do we specialise to uj = j(j + s), vj = j(j + t), where that deter-
minant collapses and the answer factors.

            9. The single shift of the Euler number family on the line t = 1
  After the double shift of Section 8 we turn to the single shift
                            Ḧn(1) = Ḧn(1) (a) = det a2i+j+1 0≤i,j≤n−1 ,
                                                             
                                                                                                                  (9.1)
the minor of the infinite Hankel matrix (ap+q ) that selects the odd rows 1, 3, . . . , 2n − 1 (rather
than the even rows 0, 2, . . . , 2n − 2 of Ḧn ). In the moment picture (3.4) the entry is a2i+j+1 =
E[z 2i · z j+1 ], so the column family is shifted from (χ0 , . . . , χn−1 ) to (χ1 , . . . , χn ), and the parity
of every column swaps: the z-type (odd) columns now form the full moment family attached to
T , while the even columns become the defective family y 1 , . . . , y n , missing y 0 . The biorthogonal
reduction M2 therefore applies with the two parities interchanged, and it is the odd part of a
that governs the determinant.
   The outcome is markedly less uniform than for Ḧn (Proposition 7.1) and for the double shift
(Section 8), both of which factor for all (s, t). The single shift (9.1) factors into linear forms
over Q on the line t = 1 (the canonical tangent fraction vj = j(j + 1); Proposition 9.1) and—
exceptionally—on the line t = 3 (Theorem 10.6); for the other integer values t = 2, 4, 5, 6, . . . an
irreducible quadratic (then cubic, . . . ) factor appears already at n = 4. On the line t = 1 every
factor is classical; at t = 3 the only departure from the t = 1 shape is a single non-classical linear
factor with non-integer root.
Proposition 9.1 (shifted determinant, t = 1). Let uj = j(j + s) and vj = j(j + 1) (so the odd
part of a is the tangent fraction). Then

   Like the double shift, the single shift is the dilated determinant of a shifted sequence, but now
                          (1)

          10. The single shift of the Euler number family on the line t = 3
  The single binomial of Lemma 9.2 is special to t = 1. Dividing the connection recurrence (3.9)
(with the row family T and the column family S ′ ) by κ̃i,m , the backward term carries the factor
                                  λTi      (2i − 1)(2i − 1 + t)(2i + t)
                                         =                              ,
                              2i(2i + 1)              2i + 1
which is a polynomial in i only when (2i + t) cancels the denominator (2i + 1), i.e. when t = 1.
Off that line no single binomial can solve the recurrence. In the first case beyond, t = 3, the
mixed connection coefficient splits into a sum of two binomials (Lemma 10.1), and this is exactly
as much structure as still closes: the evaluation is again the biorthogonal reduction M2, now
supplemented by one application of the Cauchy–Binet formula.

Remark 10.2. The second binomial is the “defect” term absent at t = 1; it vanishes only on the
diagonal (d = 0, where κ̃i,i = 1). It makes the reduction of Lemma 3.4 produce det(κ̃n̄+r,m )
as the determinant of a sum of two binomial matrices, which the single hook-content evaluation
of Lemma 7.4 no longer closes by itself. Nonetheless the determinant does close: one appli-
cation of the Cauchy–Binet formula (Lemma 10.3) turns it into a short sum of hook-content
products (Proposition 10.5), and the outcome still factors completely into linear forms over Q
(Theorem 10.6). The sole effect of the defect is to replace one classical linear factor by a single
non-classical one (the carrier Γn of Theorem 10.6), with non-integer root. Thus t = 3 is a second
factoring line beside t = 1; an irreducible higher-degree factor first occurs at the other values
t = 2, 4, 5, 6, . . . . More generally for odd t = 2r + 1 one expands Q(2r+1) in Q(1) with parameter
(1 − (2r + 1))/2
               = −r, producing an (r+1)-term sum of binomials, hence (after Cauchy–Binet) a
          n+r
sum of r hook-content products; the present case is r = 1.

while D0 is a hook-content product in b = (s − 3)/2 with positive leading coefficient. This proves
the collapse (10.5) and with it Theorem 10.6.                                                         □

Remark 12.5 (alternative evaluation via Cauchy–Binet). The determinant det D can also be
                                          (m+1)           (m+1)                     (i)
expanded directly. Writing y m+1 = a≤m+1 βa     Pa (with βm+1 = 1) and Pi′ = a≤i−1 γa Pa

and using S[Pa Pb ] = δab hSa ,
                            m+1

                 Dr,m = 2         βa(m+1) γa(n̄+r) hSa ,           i.e.      D = 2 Γ diag(hSa ) B T ,
                            a=0
             (n̄+r)             (m+1)
with Γr,a = γa      and Bm,a = βa     (0 ≤ a ≤ n). Here Cauchy–Binet is unavoidable: the inner
index a ranges over the n + 1 values 0, . . . , n (it reaches a = m + 1 = n when m = n − 1), one
more than the n rows r and columns m, so Γ and B T are rectangular and the naive factorisation
det D = 2n det Γ det diag(hSa ) det B T is meaningless. Instead,Q Cauchy–Binet      expands det D as
                                                     n                 S
                                                                         
the sum over the n-subsets S ⊂ {0, . . . , n} of 2 det(Γ:,S ) a∈S ha det(B:,S ) — one term for

Remark 13.9 (the continuous dual Hahn hierarchy). The mechanism is that S, T ∗ , S1 are consec-
utive members CDH( 12 , 21 , c), c = 0, 12 , 1, of one continuous dual Hahn hierarchy; the connection
coefficient for each half-step c 7→ c+ 21 is the same binomial (13.12) (Lemma 13.8), so the common
determinantal factor cancels and only the norms (13.10) survive.

   The proof has three steps. A one-sided reduction M2 against the even functional of the shifted
sequence (§14.1) leaves a residual determinant Rn built from the derivatives P̂i′ ; a structure
relation then replaces y P̂i′ by Ti = y P̂i′ − iP̂i and turns Rn into a square determinant of the
structure coefficients ∆i,a of the classical functional S1 (§14.2); finally, at s = 1 the closed form of
∆i,a degenerates into a rank-one factor times a Cauchy kernel, which collapses the determinant
(§14.3).
   Recall from §12 and §13 the even and odd moment functionals attached to f ,

14.1. The functionals of the doubly shifted sequence. Put dm := am+2 , so Ḧn = det(d2i+j ).

                    d2k = a2k+2 = S1 [y k ],              d2k+1 = a2k+3 = (2k + 3) S1 [y k ] =: T1∗ [y k ],

first-order operator that links S and T ∗ , shifted by 2:
                        T1∗ = S1 ◦ (2y∂y + 3),                  since         (2k + 3)y k = (2y∂y + 3)y k .         (14.3)
The decisive structural difference from the single-shift case (§13) is that here the even functional is
the classical one — S1 , the Christoffel transform of S, with data (14.2) — while the odd functional
T1∗ is non-classical for s ≥ 1; in the single-shift case the parities were reversed. Consequently the
one-sided reduction M2 is performed against S1 .

Both sides of (14.1) are thus explicit products; their equality is an elementary identity in n,
verified at n = 1 and propagated by the ratio n 7→ n + 1 (the two parities treated separately, as
in Proposition 11.1). Hence (14.1) holds for all n.                                            □
Remark 14.6 (why s = 1 is special). The mechanism contrasts sharply with the single-shift
                         (1)
case. There the ratio Ḧn /Ḧn is independent of s (Proposition 13.7), and its value is read off at
s = 0, where both functionals are classical and the connection determinants cancel. Here the ratio
  (2)
Ḧn /Ḧn genuinely depends on s — beyond linear factors (s+j) it carries a factor Qn (s) of degree
n, irreducible over Q in the computed range (e.g. Q4 = s2 + 36        169        2  44     269
                                                                7 s + 35 , Q5 = s + 9 s + 63 ) —
       ∗
and T1 remains non-classical at s = 1, so no cancellation of connection determinants is available.
What saves the evaluation is that the even functional S1 is classical for all s, so its structure
coefficients ∆i,a have the closed form (14.8); and at the special value s = 1 the linear factor of
∆i,a degenerates into a rank-one (row×column) factor times the Cauchy kernel 1/(4(i − a)2 − 1),
which collapses the determinant.

15.1. The rank-one structure.
Lemma 15.2 (rank-one structure). Write an = En+1 for the shifted Euler sequence, A =

Proof. The perturbing term s sin x contributes s σn to the coefficient sequence, so ãn = an + s σn ;
the member s = −1 is (sin3 x + 1)/ cos2 x because sin x (1 − sin2 x)/ cos2 x = sin x. The shift of
the index by 2i preserves parity and introduces a sign (−1)i : for even j both σ2i+j and σj vanish,
while for j = 2k + 1,
                             σ2i+j = σ2(i+k)+1 = (−1)i+k = (−1)i σj .

Remark 15.6 (affine dependence on s). Since the perturbation is rank one, Ḧn (fs ) is affine in s:
it equals ḦnE at s = 0 and drops by n2 ḦnE per unit of s, vanishing at the single value s = 1/ n2
(for n ≥ 2). Corollaries 27.21–27.25 record the integer members s = −2, . . . , 2.
Remark 15.7 (a perturbation that is not rank one). By contrast, the perturbation (sin x +
1)/ cos2 x − sin 2x is not rank one — it mixes two conjugate frequency pairs — and has no
product formula at all: its determinants carry irregular prime factors and change sign.

                                     16. The Springer number family
     The Springer numbers Sn are defined by
                   1         X xn
                           =  Sn ,        (Sn )n≥0 = 1, 1, 3, 11, 57, 361, 2763, 24611, . . .
             cos x − sin x       n!
                                   n≥0

(OEIS A001586); they count the type-B snakes [40] and form a moment sequence [39]. Their
dilated Hankel determinant, and that of a whole Springer number family containing them, is as
simple as that of the factorials.
   The Hankel determinants of the Springer numbers, and the equivalent continued-fraction ex-
pansions, have received considerable attention. Since (Sn ) is a moment sequence [39], its ordi-
nary Hankel determinant det(Si+j ) is a classical positive quantity accessible through the Jacobi–
Stieltjes theory of orthogonal polynomials; the associated continued fractions enumerating snakes
and cycle-alternating permutations were obtained by Josuat-Vergès [22], and the closed-form
Hankel determinants of the companion Euler numbers were established by Han [18], in parallel
with the classical evaluations of the tangent and secant (Euler) numbers going back to André [2].
What follows goes beyond the ordinary determinant: we evaluate the dilated Hankel determinant
det(a2i+j ) — and, more than that, the determinant of an entire one-parameter Springer number
family — in closed product form.
Theorem 16.1. For every integer r ≥ 1,
                              1          
                                                 2

                 Ḧn                        = t(t  + 1)     2

                       (cos x − t sin x)r                             (1 − x)r

   Idea of the proof. Write ft (x) = (cos x − t sin x)−r . We show that Ḧn (ft ) is a polynomial in
                                                    n
t of degree ≤ 3 n2 that is divisible by t(t2 + 1) ( 2 ) — it vanishes to order n2 at each of the
                                                                                   

three roots t = 0, ±i — and hence equals a constant multiple of it (Observation 3.8); a scaling
limit identifies the constant. The orders at t = 0 and at t = ±i come from two elementary
representations of ft (a rotation to an even function; an exponential series), each turning the
order into a one-line determinant expansion via the divisor method M4 of §3.4.
   We shall repeatedly use Faà di Bruno’s formula, the chain rule Q for the  n-th P derivative of a

composite g(h(x)): it expands (g ◦h)(n) as a sum of terms g (k) (h) j h(j) j with j mj = k ≤ n

   Order at t = 0. From cos x − t sin x = 1 + t2 cos(x + ϕ), ϕ = arctan t, we get ft (x) =
(1 + t2 )−r/2 secr (x + ϕ), so by the homogeneity Lemma 3.7 Ḧn (ft ) = (1 + t2 )−rn/2 Ḧn secr (· + ϕ) .

Remark 17.4. By (17.1) this determinant is, up to the scalar (s−1)−n , the single shift det(b2i+j+1 )
of the Springer sequence bm = m![xm ](cos x − sin x)−(s−1) of §16. The two degenerations are of
the kinds met earlier: s = ∞ reproduces, after rescaling, the rank-one geometric collapse met at
t = ±i in the Springer proof (the limit being ex , its symbol the Gaussian kernel of Lemma 17.3;
the same collapse reappears at m = 1 in §19.2), while the integers s = −a give an even/low-rank
collapse as in §16 (here f−a is an odd trigonometric polynomial).

                           18. The reciprocal-sine case (1 + x) x/ sin x
   The secant family of Section 11 evaluates (1 + x)/ cos x, and more generally (1 + x)/ cos(x)s+1 ,
in closed product form. It is natural to try sin in place of cos. Since sin x is odd and vanishes at
the origin, the literal analogue 1/ sin x is not a power series; the even analytic correction x/ sin x
repairs this and leads to the object of this section,
                                                           x
                                         f (x) = (1 + x)       .
                                                         sin x
Its two moment functionals turn out to be Wilson functionals sharing three of their four pa-
rameters (Propositions 18.3 and 18.5); this is what drives the evaluation, which we prove in
detail below. Unlike the cosine case, the exponent deformation does not survive: numerically,
                 s+1
(1 + x) x/ sin x      shows no comparably nice dilated determinant once s ̸= 0, so we study only
the member s = 0 displayed above. Write g(x) = x/ sin x and
                    bk = (2k)! [x2k ]g        (b0 = 1, b1 = 13 , b2 = 15
                                                                      7
                                                                         , b3 = 31
                                                                                21 , . . . ).
54                                            GUO-NIU HAN

Since g is even and x g is odd, the moments of f are
                                 a2k = bk ,     a2k+1 = (2k + 1) bk ,                            (18.1)
so the two functionals attached to the quadratic decomposition (the even S and the odd T of
Section 3.2) are
                          S[y k ] = bk ,   T [y k ] = (2k + 1) bk = T ∗ [y k ].
The situation is the mirror image of the cosine family of Section 11: here the odd functional T
is the classical one — and, less obviously, the even functional S turns out to be classical as well,
indeed itself a Wilson functional (Proposition 18.5). The purpose of this section is to prove the
following evaluation.
Theorem 18.1 (closed form for (1 + x) x/ sin x). Set
                                                           6
                                144 · 64 k (k!)5 (k + 1)! (2k)! (2k + 1)!

Then the determinants obey the two-step recurrence Ḧn+2 = 23 Q(n) Ḧn (n ≥ 0), and hence are
the products of factorials
                                 −1
                            N NY                      N NY −1
                   Ḧ2N = 32        Q(2k),    Ḧ2N +1 = 23        Q(2k + 1).           (18.3)

   The proof occupies the remainder of the section; its plan is as follows. Subsections 18.2 and 18.3
make the two functionals explicit: T is the Wilson functional with parameters ( 21 , 12 , 12 , 21 ), and
— the decisive structural fact — S is itself Wilson, with parameters ( 12 , 21 , 12 , 0) (the Wilson and
continuous Hahn families used throughout are recalled in §18.1). Subsection 18.4 applies the
biorthogonal reduction M2, which confines the whole evaluation to a determinant of connection
coefficients κi,m between the two families. Because the families differ in a single Wilson parameter,
κ collapses to a single hypergeometric term (Subsection 18.5), and the connection determinant
becomes, after removal of row and column factors, a determinant of products of two Catalan
numbers; that Catalan determinant is evaluated in closed form by Desnanot–Jacobi condensation
(Subsection 18.6). Subsection 18.7 assembles the pieces.
18.1. The Wilson and continuous Hahn functionals. Both functionals of this section belong
to the top two levels of the Askey scheme of hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials [25, 21]; we
recall the two families and fix the notation used below.
Wilson. For parameters (a, b, c, d) with positive real parts (or occurring in conjugate pairs), the
Wilson functional Wa,b,c,d acts on polynomials in the variable y = x2 by

or a specialisation of them. In this section the two functionals are Wilson functionals sharing
                                  DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                               55

a = b = c = 12 and differing only in the fourth parameter d — d = 12 for the odd functional T ,
d = 0 for the even functional S; substituting these into the formulas above (in the rescaled variable
y = 4x2 of Proposition 18.3) reproduces the recurrence data of §18.2–18.3. This single-parameter
difference is what collapses the connection coefficients in §18.5.
Continuous Hahn. One level below sit the continuous Hahn polynomials [25, §9.4], orthogonal
on R in the variable x with weight Γ(a + ix) Γ(b + ix) Γ(c − ix) Γ(d − ix) (parameters of positive
real part, with c = ā, d = b̄), and given by
                                                                                           
                              n (a + c)n (a + d)n       −n, n + a + b + c + d − 1, a + ix

                                       n!                         a + c, a + d

When the weight is even — as for a = b = c = d = 12 , where it equals |Γ( 21 + ix)|4 = π 2 sech2 πx
— the monic polynomials are symmetric, pn+1 = x pn − βn pn−1 with βn rational in n, so odd
and even degrees decouple. Pushing the measure forward under the quadratic map y = x2 then
turns them into a Wilson family: this is precisely the quadratic decomposition of §3.2. It is why
the even functional S wears two faces, each used at a different point below: continuous Hahn in
x, which yields its clean S-fraction un = n4 /(4n2 − 1) (Proposition 18.4), and Wilson in y = x2 ,
which yields its connection coefficients against T (Proposition 18.5).

  Of this data, the sequel uses the recurrence coefficients cTm , λTm (in the connection recurrence

18.3. The even functional S. Contrary to a first impression, the even functional S is also
elementary: its Stieltjes S-fraction is as clean as can be, the single rational coefficient un =
n4 /(4n2 − 1). The orthogonal-polynomial recurrence (the J-fraction) is then fixed by the even
contraction (§2.6) as cSi = u2i + u2i+1 , λSi = u2i−1 u2i — explicit, but bulkier than un itself, which
is why we work from the S-fraction.

  Thus the two monic families Pi and ρm attached to f are Wilson families differing in a single
parameter : d = 0 for the even functional, d = 12 for the odd one. This is the structural fact
behind the closed form of the connection coefficients in Subsection 18.5 below.

The first connection coefficients are κi,i = 1, κi,i−1 = 4i−1 and κi,i−2 = − (4i−3)(4i−2)(4i−1) .
   The prefactor in (18.11) being a known factorial product, Theorem 18.1 is now reduced to the
evaluation of the connection determinant det(κn̄+r, m ) — a purely algebraic identity, in which
every entry is an explicitly generated rational number and all of the analytic data (η-values, the
sech2 and Wilson weights) has been discharged.
   At first sight this determinant looks intractable: a connection array between two distinct
classical families is in general a balanced 3 F2 -type double sum, the matrix in (18.11) is neither
of Hankel nor of Toeplitz type, and — unlike the double shift of the cosine family (Section 14)
— no free parameter is in sight. The present pair is closer than it looks: by Propositions 18.3
and 18.5 the two families are Wilson families differing in a single parameter (d = 0 versus d = 12 ),
and for a one-parameter change the connection coefficients collapse to a single hypergeometric
term (Lemma 18.6 below). After removing row and column factors, the connection determinant
becomes the Hadamard product of a Toeplitz and a Hankel matrix of Catalan numbers, and that
determinant is closed under Desnanot–Jacobi condensation once its two shifts are treated as free
parameters (Theorem 18.8).

Remark 18.9 (the two-parameter family is essential). The condensation closes only because the
two-parameter family (18.17) is considered: the four corner minors of FN (a, b) shift (a, b) in four
different directions, and no one-parameter subfamily (in particular not the diagonal b = a + 1
that the application requires) is stable under (18.20). The hypotheses a ≥ N − 1 and b ≥ a + 1 are
what the condensation argument needs, keeping all five smaller determinants of (18.20) within
the range of the induction; the application uses only the diagonal b = a + 1.
                                          DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                             61

Remark 18.10 (the collapse mechanism). The proof is an instance of the two-stage collapse mech-
anism of the reduction M2 described at the end of Section 3.2. The single-term connection
(Lemma 18.6) is the analogue, on the Wilson level, of the classical fact that connection co-
efficients between two Jacobi families sharing one parameter factor completely; and the kernel
Ci−m−1 Ci+m it leaves behind generates the two-parameter Catalan family (18.17), which is stable
under Desnanot–Jacobi condensation. In particular, no deformation parameter and no recourse
to the divisor method M4 is needed.

                     19. An elliptic deformation of the Euler numbers
    The Hankel determinants attached to elliptic functions have been studied by Thomas Jan
Stieltjes [42], Philippe Flajolet [12], Hubert Prodinger [37] and Dominique Dumont [9]. Stielt-
jes computed the continued fractions — equivalently the Hankel determinants — of many spe-
cial series; Flajolet gave the combinatorial theory relating Hankel determinants, J-fractions and
weighted lattice paths; Dumont interpreted the Taylor coefficients of the Jacobi elliptic func-
tions combinatorially; and Prodinger obtained the continued fractions of several tangent- and
secant-type deformations.
    Recall the Jacobi elliptic functions sn(x, m), cn(x, m), dn(x, m), of modulus parameter m =
                                                                      Rϕ             −1/2
k . Writing the incomplete elliptic integral of the first kind u = 0 1 − m sin2 θ
  2                                                                                        dθ, they
are defined by inverting u 7→ ϕ and setting sn(u, m) = sin ϕ, cn(u, m) = cos ϕ, dn(u, m) =
(1 − m sin2 ϕ)1/2 ; they are doubly periodic meromorphic functions satisfying sn2 + cn2 = 1 and
dn2 +m sn2 = 1, and reduce to sin x, cos x, 1 at m = 0 (Whittaker–Watson [49, Ch. 22]).
    Replacing sin, cos by the Jacobi elliptic functions sn(x, m), cn(x, m) deforms the Euler gener-
ating function sec x + tan x = (1 + sin x)/ cos x into
                                        1 + sn(x, m) X       xn
                      gm (x) :=                     =  En (m) ,                        En (0) = En ,          (19.1)

2 − m, E4 = 5 − 4m, E5 = 16 − 16m + m2 ).

19.2. The degree bound (A), via the reciprocal modulus. By Jacobi’s reciprocal-modulus

Remark 19.2 (why the leading symbol is not enough). One might hope to replace G by eϵ sin x (its
ϵ → 0 symbol) and argue that am agrees with m![xm ]eϵ sin x to relative order 2. This is false from
the per-entry orders alone: for n ≥ 6 a perturbation of each am by a term of order ordϵ am + 2
can lower ordϵ Ḧn below n2 (numerically, to 13 at n = 6 and 17 at n = 7). The argument above
instead uses the genuine moment structure (19.4)–(19.7) of G, which forces ordϵ cν ≥ |ν|; that is
what makes it correct.
19.3. Vanishing at m = 1 (B), via the imaginary transformation. At m = 1, sn(x, 1) =
tanh x, cn(x, 1) = sech x, so g1 (x) = (1 + tanh x) cosh x = ex : a rank-one geometric sequence.

Remark 19.3. The elliptic proof draws on classical Jacobi-function theory — the reciprocal-
modulus and imaginary transformations, the Fourier/nome expansions of sn, cn, and the nome
order ordϵ γp ≥ |p| − 1 of the coefficients of cn / dn [49, §22.6] — as cited inputs; everything else
(the ODE (19.3) and recurrence (19.7), the order counts) is elementary. Its skeleton is identical to
the Springer proof: m = 1 (where g1 = ex ) plays the role of t = ±i; the imaginary transformation,
turning m into 1−m, plays the role of the twist t 7→ i that produced t2 +1; the reciprocal modulus
(m → ∞) plays the role of t → ∞; and m = 0 fixes the constant. The structural novelty is that
the exponential expansions are two-sided (Lemma 3.9) and that, for the degree bound, the lower
order ordϵ cν ≥ |ν| must be read from G’s moment structure, not from per-entry orders. The
further deformation (1 + t sn)/ cn retains this ladder form when t ̸= 1.

                          20. An algebraic family: (1 + x)/(1 − x2 )s/2
   Replacing cos(x)−(s+1) by the algebraic even series (1 − x2 )−s/2 = k≥0 −s/2        (−1)k x2k gives
                                                                        P            
                                                                                  k
another one-parameter family with the same closed-product behaviour. A scale parameter t, i.e.
(1 − tx2 )−s/2 , would be inessential: it multiplies the moment a2i+j by t i+⌊j/2⌋ and hence Ḧn by
                 n        2
the constant t( 2 )+⌊(n−1) /4⌋ , so we set t = 1.
   Notably, neither of the two moment functionals S[y k ] = a2k and T ∗ [y k ] = a2k+1 is classical
here: the even moments µk = 4k ( 21 )k ( 2s )k form a Hadamard product of two Laguerre moment
sequences, and the three-term recurrence coefficients of both S and T ∗ are rational, but not
polynomial, functions of s. Accordingly the proof below uses no orthogonal polynomials at all: it
rests on an elementary contiguous relation in s (M5), which relates Ḧn (s) to Ḧn (s − 2) by column
operations and is solved by a periodicity argument, anchored by a Vandermonde evaluation M1
at the single point s = 2 (Step 3 of the proof) — M1 is thus only the base case, not the mechanism
that carries the result across all s.

                    21. The squared algebraic family (1 + x)2 /(1 − x2 )s/2
   The prefactor 1 + x of Theorem 20.1 can be squared. The resulting family is not a reparametri-
sation of the previous one: writing (1 + x)2 (1 − x2 )−s/2 = (1 + x)2−s/2 (1 − x)−s/2 , a rescaling
x 7→ βx must preserve the singular pair {±1}, hence β = ±1, and matching the exponents at ∓β
against (1 + x)1−σ/2 (1 − x)−σ/2 forces two incompatible values of σ for either sign. The deter-
minant confirms that the family is new: the product form survives except in its topmost factor,
where (s/2)n−1 deforms into a binomial expression carrying the denominator s − 3. The excluded
value s = 3 is precisely the point where this binomial degenerates into odd harmonic numbers
(Remark 21.3). The proof below runs the contiguous step of M5 once, from s to s − 2, and closes
it by a Cauchy–Binet expansion instead of the periodicity argument; no anchoring special value is
                                                                         (r)
needed. The same machinery evaluates all the shifted determinants Ḧn = det(a2i+j+r ) of both
families; this is the subject of the next section (Section 22), where those of the algebraic family
turn out to be pure products for every shift r, while those of the squared algebraic family split by
the parity of r — odd shifts erase the defect entirely, even shifts carry the same s − 3 binomial.
   Throughout the section, am = am (s) and µk = µk (s) = (2k)!  k! (s/2)k are the moments of (1 +

cancel exactly, the denominator s − 3 disappears, and the pure product of Theorem 20.1 drops

the diagonal from s − 3 to s − 4: the mismatch with the denominator s − 3 of det G1̂ ruins the
cancellation, and the binomial numerator of (21.1) is the trace it leaves.

                 22. Shifted determinants of the two algebraic families

For the algebraic family the answer is a pure product for every r, and the proof is a direct

s − 3 binomial of Theorem 21.1 survives. Throughout, ρ := ⌊r/2⌋.

Remark 22.2 (a third proof of Theorem 20.1). The case r = 0 of Proposition 22.1 is Theorem 20.1
itself: the algebraic family is, after all, within reach of M1. Its term ratio has degree one in each
parity class — which places it outside the Beta family of Section 4 and defeats the classical
Hankel treatment — but the dilated rows sit on even indices and never mix the two classes, so
the row factorisation (3.1) holds with monic Qj of degree j. The three proofs illuminate different
structure: M5 the contiguous relation in s, Remark 21.2 the exact double-factorial cancellation,
and M1 the two-branch Beta pattern.

                   23. A Bessel analogue of the Euler number family
The Bessel cosine cosbs . For s > −1 define the Bessel cosine of order s as the normalised

Corollary 23.2 (smooth and degenerate offsets). Let s, t > − 12 .
       (i) If t − s ∈ Z, then Ḧn (fs,t ) = 0 precisely for n ≥ 2(t − s) + 1 when t > s, for n ≥ 2 when
           t = s, and for n ≥ 2(s − t) + 2 when t < s.
      (ii) If t − s ∈/ Z, then Ḧn (fs,t ) ̸= 0 for every n, and (23.4) is a closed product of linear
           forms. For half-integer offset t − s ∈ 12 + Z the signed factor moreover evaluates in double
           factorials by Lemma 7.5, applied with the odd argument δ = 2(t − s).
                                     DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                          71


Proof. For s, t > − 12 every Pochhammer symbol in (23.4) is a product of positive reals, so Ḧn = 0
if and only if the double product vanishes, i.e. if and only if t − s = r − c for some 1 ≤ r ≤ n̄,
1 ≤ c ≤ n. This forces t − s ∈ Z, and conversely, for t − s ∈ Z, such (r, c) exist if and only if
n̄ ≥ t − s + 1 and n ≥ s − t + 1, i.e. n ≥ max 2(t − s) + 1, 2(s − t) + 2, 2 , which is the stated
threshold in each of the three cases.                                                            □
