Remark 23.4 (the boundary of the ladder). The closed form also quantifies the collapse at the
edge of the order ladder. At s = − 21 the numerators (s + 12 )l vanish for every l ≥ 1, so Ḧn = 0
as soonR as n̄ ≥ 2, i.e. for all n ≥ 3; at t = − 21 likewise Ḧn = 0 for all n ≥ 4 (n ≥ 2). Thus
         x
cos x + 0 J0 degenerates from n = 3 and J0 (x) + sin x from n = 4, although both pairs have half-
integer offset: rule (ii) of Corollary 23.2 genuinely needs s, t > − 12 . More generally (s + 12 )l = 0
for l > k at s = − 12 − k, giving Ḧn = 0 for n ≥ 2k + 3. The explanation is visible in the Poisson
formula: at s = − 12 the weight (1 − t2 )s−1/2 ceases to be a measure and cos x is the characteristic
function of the atomic two-point measure at ±1 — a rank collapse, the same degeneracy as the
(1 + x) cos-type atomic cases elsewhere in the paper.
   The proof of Theorem 23.1 occupies the next three subsections: the orthogonal data of the
family are explicit (Lemma 23.5), the connection coefficients collapse to a single term because the
two functionals differ in one parameter (Lemma 23.6), and the residual connection determinant
— which, unlike the binomial determinant of the trigonometric family, carries the parameters s, t
in its entries — is evaluated by Desnanot–Jacobi condensation (Theorem 23.7), in the manner of
the Catalan determinant of Section 18.6.
23.1. Orthogonal data. By (23.3) the even part of a involves only s and the odd part only
t, through one and the same one-parameter moment sequence; we record the data for the even
part, the odd part being identical with t in place of s. All computations take place over the field
Q(s, t).

23.2. Connection coefficients.
                            Pi     As in Section 3.2 let Pi and Qm be the monic orthogonal polyno-
mials of S and T , and Pi = m=0 κi,m Qm the connection expansion. Because the two functionals

                                      δ := t − s,          d := i − m.
Lemma 23.6 (connection coefficients). For 0 ≤ m ≤ i,
                                                                       
               (2i)!      δ(δ − 1) · · · (δ − d + 1)     (2i)!         δ             1
     κi,m =         d

            (2m)! 4 d! (t + 2m + 1)d (s + i + m)d      (2m)! 4d        d (t + 2m + 1)d (s + i + m)d

Proof. Write ki,m for the right-hand side, extended by ki,m = 0 for m < 0 or m > i. By Lemma 3.3
the array κ is the unique solution of the recurrence (3.9) with row κ0,0 = 1, κ0,m = 0 (m ̸= 0); the
candidate has the same row i = 0 (at d = 0 every product in (23.7) is empty), so it suffices to show
that k satisfies (3.9). Every factor of (23.7) is nonzero on the triangle 0 ≤ m ≤ i over Q(s, t), and
telescoping each block gives the four neighbour ratios as rational functions of (m, d, s, t) (recall
i = m + d):
                      ki+1,m          (2i + 1)(2i + 2) (δ − d) (s + i + m)
                             =                                                 ,
                       ki,m    4 (d + 1) (t + 2m + d + 1) (s + 2i)(s + 2i + 1)
                      ki,m−1          (2m − 1)(2m) (δ − d) (t + 2m + d)

                       ki,m    4 (d + 1) (t + 2m − 1)(t + 2m) (s + i + m − 1)

Dividing (3.9) by ki,m and inserting these ratios together with the recurrence data of Lemma 23.5
— via Lemma 3.5, cSi = u2i + u2i+1 , λSi = u2i−1 u2i , cTm = v2m + v2m+1 , λTm+1 = v2m+1 v2m+2 —
turns the recurrence into a rational-function identity in (m, d, s, t), verified by clearing denomi-
nators. The boundary columns take care of themselves: at m = 0 the second ratio carries the
factor 2m and vanishes, as required by κi,−1 = 0; at m = i the third and fourth ratios carry the
factor d and vanish, as required by κi,i+1 = 0 and κi−1,i = 0; and at m = i + 1 the recurrence
reduces to ki+1,i+1 = ki,i = 1.                                                                   □

   The binomial dδ in (23.7) is the source of the integer-offset degeneracy: for δ = t − s ∈ Z≥0
                   
the connection array is banded, κi,m = 0 for i − m > δ, and the residual block of Lemma 3.4
eventually has a zero diagonal. The next theorem makes this quantitative — and evaluates the
determinant for every (s, t) at once.

23.3. The connection determinant. Unlike its trigonometric counterpart (Lemma 7.4), the
kernel left over after removing row and column factors from (23.7) depends on i + m as well as
on i − m, so the dual Jacobi–Trudi identity does not apply. Instead we evaluate, more generally,
every minor of the connection array on consecutive rows and consecutive columns: the two extra
shift parameters make the family stable under Desnanot–Jacobi condensation, exactly as for the
Catalan determinant of the reciprocal-sine function (Section 18.6).
                                   DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                        73

Theorem 23.7 (connection determinant). Let p ≥ a ≥ 0 and N ≥ 0 be integers and put q = p−a.

The proof is by induction on N through the Desnanot–Jacobi identity (2.1), in the manner of the
condensation proofs of the determinant calculus [26].
   For N = 0 both sides equal 1. For q = 0 (any N ) the matrix (κp+r, p+m ) is unitriangular
(κi,m = 0 for m > i, κi,i = 1), so D = 1 = G, every product in (23.8) being empty. For N = 1,
                                        (2p)! 4−q δ(δ − 1) · · · (δ − q + 1)

by (23.7). Let N ≥ 2 and q ≥ 1. Deleting the first or last row and the first or last column of
the matrix of D(p, a, N ) shifts (p, a) and N inside the same family, so its four corner minors are
D(p + 1, a + 1, N − 1), D(p, a, N − 1), D(p + 1, a, N − 1), D(p, a + 1, N − 1), and its central minor
is D(p + 1, a + 1, N − 2); the Desnanot–Jacobi identity reads
  D(p, a, N ) D(p + 1, a + 1, N − 2)
         = D(p + 1, a + 1, N − 1) D(p, a, N − 1) − D(p + 1, a, N − 1) D(p, a + 1, N − 1). (23.10)
The five smaller determinants have offsets q, q, q + 1, q − 1 ≥ 0 and sizes N − 1, N − 2, so they
equal the corresponding G’s by the induction hypothesis, and G(p + 1, a + 1, N − 2) ̸= 0, every
factor of (23.9) being a nonzero element of Q(s, t). Hence (23.10) determines D(p, a, N ), and it
remains to check that G satisfies the same relation, i.e. that R1 − R2 = 1 for

Remark 23.8 (the parallel with the trigonometric family). The parallel with Proposition 7.1 is
                                                                                      n̄ Q
exact, with one systematic change of scale. Both formulas share the skeleton (−1)( 2 ) i<n (2i)!
times a signed double product; but the trigonometric signed factor is Ω(t − s) while the Bessel
one is Ω(2(t − s)): the Bessel ladder walks in half steps. The closed double-factorial evaluations
of Lemma 7.5 therefore apply on the lines t − s ∈ 21 + Z (where 2(t − s) is odd), exactly as
they apply to the trigonometric family on the lines t − s odd; and the forced roots of Ω produce
the degeneration on t − s ∈ Z, as they do there on t − s even. The parameter blocks, by
contrast, are reciprocal: (s + 1)n−1+l in the denominator here versus (s + 1)2l in the numerator
there, reflecting moments that are bounded (characteristic functions of probability measures)
rather than factorially growing. Both proofs are instances of the collapse mechanism of M2: two
functionals of a single classical hierarchy differing in one parameter — Jacobi–Gegenbauer here,
                                  DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                         75


secant-type there, Wilson for the reciprocal-sine function. The kernel dδ / (t+2m+1)d (s+i+m)d
                                                                                                     

of (23.7) sits between the two known extremes: like the pure binomial (t−s)/2
                                                                                     
                                                                                 d     of Lemma 7.3 it
is binomial in i − m, but like the Catalan kernel of the reciprocal-sine family it also depends on
i + m — which is why the finishing step is condensation rather than Jacobi–Trudi. Finally,       R xfor
half-integer s, t the members of (23.2) are elementary — e.g. (s, t) = ( 12 , 0) gives sin(x)/x + 0 J0
— but for general order they are genuine Bessel functions, so the family lies strictly outside
the scope of Section 7: this is not a reparametrisation of a known family. Consistently with
the collapse mechanism, the crossbreeds mixing the trigonometric and Bessel hierarchies — one
functional secant-type, the other Gegenbauer — were tested numerically and are never smooth:
the single-hierarchy hypothesis of M2 is not ornamental.

            25. A multiplicative Bessel family: (1 + x) cosbν2 at even orders
                                 Rx
  The additive family cosbs + 0 cosbt of Section 23 is the Bessel analogue of the Euler (s, t)
family. The Bessel cosine introduced there,
                              2 ν          X     (−1)k
        cosbν (x) = Γ(ν + 1)        Jν (x) =                  x2k , cosbν (0) = 1,     (25.1)
                               x               4k k! (ν + 1)k
                                                k≥0

also produces an analogue of the secant family (1 + x)/ cos(x)s+1 of Section 11, with the exponent
ladder replaced once more by the order ladder: the multiplicative family
                                      fν (x) = (1 + x) cosbν (x)2 .                                   (25.2)
(The parameter cannot sit as a power on the function side: as recorded in Section 23, no fixed h
gives cosbν = h−(ν+1) ; and it is the square of the even factor that carries the structure, through
the product formula of Lemma 25.1 below.) At ν = 21 the member (25.2) is (1 + x)(sin x/x)2 ; at
the boundary order ν = − 21 it is (1 + x) cos2 x.
   The determinants Ḧn (fν ) display a sharp parity contrast: at even orders n = 2N they factor
completely into linear forms in ν, while at odd orders an irreducible factor of growing degree
appears. This section proves the even half in closed form (Theorem 25.4); the odd orders, which
remain open, are discussed in Remark 25.7.
                                     DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                                  77

   The method deserves a word in advance. The even moment functional of fν is not classical
— it is the self-convolution of the symmetric Beta law, not an orthogonality weight — so the
biorthogonal collapse M2 that evaluates the secant family and the additive Bessel family is out
of reach. What replaces it is the most elementary reduction of the paper: the Vandermonde
method M1 of Section 3.1, applied here for the only time beyond the Beta family. Watson’s
classical product formula makes the even moments a single hypergeometric term; the (1 + x)-
coupling preserves this; and for even n — and only for even n — the resulting row factorisation
lands exactly on the Vandermonde degree bound. No orthogonal polynomials appear, and quasi-
definiteness is never invoked.
25.1. The moments. Write am = m! [xm ]fν as usual.

wi Q2m+1 (i), which is (25.6). The degrees in (25.5) are l + l + (N − 1 − l) + (N − 1 − l) = 2N − 2
and 2N − 1, both ≤ n − 1; so the matrix factors,

Corollary 25.5 (complete factorisation and the zero locus). For every N ≥ 1 the determinant
Ḧ2N (fν ) is a nonzero rational function of ν that factors completely into linear forms over Q: a
closed product form at every even order. Its numerator zeros lie at negative half-integers (from
(ν + 21 )k ) and at the points ν = − 14 − 2j , j ≥ 0 (from (2ν + 12 )k ); its poles at negative integers. In
the Poisson range ν > − 21 :

   The values in Corollary 25.6 have numerators that are pure powers of two and odd denomina-
tors. The member (1 + x)(sin x/x)2 sits one step from the reciprocal-sine function of Section 18:
there the sine is downstairs, (1 + x) x/ sin x, the functionals are Wilson, and the evaluation runs
through M2 and condensation; here the sine is upstairs and squared, the functional is not classical
at all, and the evaluation is pure Vandermonde — yet only the even orders survive.
Remark 25.7 (odd orders: where M1 stops). At an odd order n = 2N + 1 the even column indices
reach l = N , so the padding (25.4) must be lengthened to (ν + 1 + i)N (2ν + 1 + i)N , and then
                      deg Q2l = 2N = n − 1,                deg Q2m+1 = 2N + 1 = n > n − 1 :
the odd columns overflow the Vandermonde degree bound of Lemma 3.1 by exactly one, and M1
yields no evaluation — for the dilated determinant of (25.2) the method works precisely at even
orders. This failure is structural, not merely technical. The even half of the parity contrast is
Theorem 25.4; the odd half remains open.

                     26. Connection with a determinant of Chapoton–Han
   The dilated Hankel determinant of this paper was first met by the author while studying
a conjecture from his joint work with Chapoton on the roots of the Poupard and Kreweras
polynomials [4]. This section closes the circle: after a change of basis, that conjecture is the
dilated Hankel determinant of (1 + x)/ cos x evaluated in Proposition 11.1.
26.1. The evaluation ρ and the conjecture. Call the index of a nonzero polynomial its
degree plus its valuation, and call P palindromic of index d if xd P (1/x) = P (x) — for example
P = xi (1 + x)j , of index 2i + j. For such P the polynomial (xd + 1)P (1) − 2P (x) vanishes to
second order at x = 1 (palindromy gives P ′ (1) = d2 P (1)), so the index-lowering operator of [4,
§5],
                                           (xd + 1) P (1) − 2P (x)
                                 N0 (P ) =                         ,
                                                   (x − 1)2
is a polynomial, palindromic of index d − 2 when nonzero. Iterating ⌊d/2⌋ times lands in index
at most 1, and, following [4], the evaluation ρ is the final value of this iteration:
                                                                            ⌊d/2⌋
                                          ρ(P ) := constant term of N0              (P );
                                      DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                          81

              ρ(1) = 1 and ρ c(1 + x) = c. Since P = (x − 1)2 Q has P (1) = 0, giving N0 (x −
                                     
in particular
1)2 Q = −2Q, and since ρ(P ) = ρ(N0 P ) by definition, the map ρ obeys, for palindromic Q,
     

                                ρ (x − 1)2 Q(x) = −2 ρ Q(x) .
                                                            
                                                                                         (26.1)
Note that ρ is linear on each fixed index but is not a linear functional on all of Q[x]: for example
ρ (1 + x)xk ̸= ρ(xk ) + ρ(xk+1 ), the three indices 2k + 1, 2k, 2k + 2 being distinct. Every
manipulation below stays within a single index, on which ρ is linear. Put
                          Ak := ρ(xk ),    Bk := ρ (1 + x)xk
                                                               
                                                                     (k ≥ 0),
so that (Ak ) = 1, 1, 2, 10, 104, 1816, . . . are the generalised Euler numbers of type 2n (OEIS
A005799) and (Bk /2k ) = 1, 1, 2, 8, 56, 608, . . . are the Genocchi medians (OEIS A005439). Fol-
lowing [4], let MN be the N × N matrix
                     MN (i, j) = ρ xi (1 + x)j 2−⌊j/2⌋ ,
                                                  
                                                               0 ≤ i, j ≤ N − 1.            (26.2)
The conjecture in question [4, Conj. 5.4] asserts that
                                      N −1

In particular [4, Conj. 5.4] holds.
   The proof has three steps. First, a triangular change of column basis, powered by the reduction
(26.1), strips the powers of 1 + x: det MN is an explicit power of 2 times the dilated Hankel
determinant of the sequence interleaving (Ak ) and (Bk ) (Theorem 26.3). Second — the key step
— we prove a moment representation of ρ (Lemma 26.5): on each index, ρ is the secant functional
S (even index) or the functional T ∗ (odd index) of Section 11, composed with an explicit change
of variable; in particular Ak = S[((1 + y)/2)k ] and Bk = T ∗ [((1 + y)/2)k ]. This is where the
secant numbers enter; the identification was observed, conjecturally, in [4, §5]. Third, the affine
substitution y = 2v − 1 transports the orthogonal data of Section 11 (Lemma 26.6), and the
biorthogonal reduction M2 runs exactly as in the proof of Proposition 11.1. In passing, the first
step also proves Conjecture 5.3 of [4] (Remark 26.4).

   We prove (26.8) by induction on e. For e = 0 both sides equal the constant π (resp. σ, since
ρ(c(1 + x)) = c and T ∗ [1] = 1). For e ≥ 1, since ρ(P ) = ρ(N0 P ), it suffices to show that the
right-hand sides are unchanged when (e, π) is replaced by (e − 1, π̃), and (e, σ) by (e − 1, σ̃).
Writing ζ ∗ := −4/(1 + y), so that 1/ζ ∗ = −(1 + y)/4,

  Thus the root conjecture of Chapoton–Han and the Euler dilated Hankel determinant are one
and the same evaluation: the change of column basis of Theorem 26.3 strips the powers of 1 + x,
and the substitution y = 2v − 1 of Lemma 26.6 carries what remains onto Proposition 11.1.

                                 27. Corollaries: explicit evaluations
   This section collects the explicit members of the families evaluated in this paper. No new
proofs are needed: each corollary is a substitution of parameters into the theorem quoted at the
head of its subsection, recording the generating function f , the determinant Ḧn (f ) and, where a
                                                         (1)                  (2)
closed form is available, the single and double shifts Ḧn = Ḧn (f ′ ) and Ḧn = Ḧn (f ′′ ).
27.1. The Beta family am = ρm (α)m /(β)m . Every member of the Beta family (4.1) is a plain
sequence am = ρm (α)m /(β)m (we normalise a0 = 1), with term ratio am+1 /am = ρ (m + α)/(m +
β); when the denominator parameter β is absent the sequence is ρm (α)m . By Corollary 4.3
(resp. Proposition 4.2 when β is absent) the dilated Hankel determinant Ḧn = det(a2i+j ) is a
closed product of factorials. The shifted sequence am+1 is again a Beta member, with (ρ, α, β) 7→
                                          (1)
(ρ, α + 1, β + 1), so the single shift Ḧn := det(a2i+j+1 ) is likewise closed. We record several
                                                                      (1)
well-known members with their (ρ, α, β), giving both Ḧn and Ḧn .

record the specialisations c ∈ {1, −1}, in each case giving f and its derivative f ′ .

27.3. The Euler number family. The cases s < 0, t < 0, or t − s even are trivial; assume
henceforth s ≥ 0, t ≥ 0 and δ = t − s odd. Throughout n̄ = ⌈n/2⌉, n = ⌊n/2⌋. With these,
Proposition 7.1 evaluates Ḧn in closed form for every (s, t); its signed factor Ω(δ) (δ = t − s) is
evaluated for δ ∈ {±1, ±3} by Lemma 7.5.
   Each corollary below records both f and, for the double shift, its second derivative f ′′ : the
doubly shifted sequence (Fk+2 )k≥0 has exponential generating function f ′′ , and by Proposition 8.4
                       (2)
the determinant Ḧn (f ) = Ḧn (f ′′ ) is the unshifted one times the explicit positive scalar (2n −

                         28. The Lindström–Gessel–Viennot approach
   The determinant Ḧn counts something, and the natural first attempt at a combinatorial eval-
uation is the Lindström–Gessel–Viennot (LGV) lemma [30, 13, 23], which turns a determinant of
path counts into a signed sum over non-intersecting path families. We recall the lemma, use it to
see why the classical Hankel determinant is a clean product, and then read off the single change
that the dilation makes. In the end this route did not deliver our evaluations — the algebraic
methods M1–M6 did — but it locates precisely where the difficulty enters, and we record it for
orientation.

28.1. Hankel minors as path systems. It is convenient to write, for finite index sets R =
{r0 < · · · < rn−1 } and C = {c0 < · · · < cn−1 },
                                                       
                                 H(R; C) := det ari +cj 0≤i,j≤n−1 ,

the corresponding minor of the infinite Hankel matrix (ap+q ). In this notation the classical and
dilated determinants are
                                                                                                           
 Hn = H {0, 1, . . . , n − 1}; {0, 1, . . . , n − 1} , Ḧn = H {0, 2, . . . , 2n − 2}; {0, 1, . . . , n − 1} :
they share the column set {0, 1, . . . , n − 1} and differ only in that the row (“top”) indices are
dilated from 0, 1, . . . , n − 1 to 0, 2, . . . , 2n − 2 — exactly the even-row selection of Definition 1.1.
Lemma 28.1 (Lindström–Gessel–Viennot [30, 13]). Let D be a locally finitePacyclic directed
                                                                                      Q           graph
with edge weights in a commutative ring, and for vertices u, v let e(u, v) = P : u→v edges of P w
be the weight generating function of directed paths. For source and sink tuples A = (A0 , . . . , An−1 )
and B = (B0 , . . . , Bn−1 ),
                                                             X                    n−1
                                                                                  Y
                                        
                         det e(Ai , Bj ) 0≤i,j≤n−1 =                     sgn(σ)         w(Pk ),
                                                       (P0 ,...,Pn−1 )            k=0

the sum over families in which Pk runs from Ak to Bσ(k) for some permutation σ and the Pk are
pairwise vertex-disjoint. If the endpoints are nonpermutable — every vertex-disjoint family has
σ = id — the determinant is the subtraction-free weight of the non-intersecting families joining
Ak to Bk .
   Now realise the moments as paths. If (ak ) are the moments of a quasi-definite functional
with J-fraction coefficients (bh , λh ) — the (cn , λn ) of Section 2.5, renamed here because c denotes
column indices — then by Flajolet’s theory [12, 46] ak is the weight of all Motzkin paths of length
k from height 0 to height 0, a level step at height h carrying weight bh and a down-step from
height h weight λh . Place a source Ai on the horizontal axis at abscissa −ri and a sink Bj at
abscissa cj ; a directed path Ai → Bj is then a Motzkin path of length ri + cj that stays weakly
above the axis, so e(Ai , Bj ) = ari +cj and
                                                              
                                 H(R; C) = det e(Ai , Bj ) 0≤i,j≤n−1 .

Since all sources lie at abscissa ≤ 0 and all sinks at ≥ 0, the endpoints are nonpermutable: the
only vertex-disjoint families are the nested ones joining Ai to Bi (Figures 1 and 2). Hence every
such minor H(R; C) is a subtraction-free count of non-intersecting Motzkin paths.
                                      DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                        95

28.2. What the dilation changes. For the classical Hn (Figure 1) the two index sets coincide,
R = C = {0, 1, . . . , n − 1}: the sources and sinks are equally spaced and symmetric about the
origin, the nested family fills every level in turn, and its weight telescopes into the Heilermann
product Hn = λ1n−1 λ2n−2 · · · λn−1 [20, 46] (here a0 = 1). This is the combinatorial face of the
J-fraction, and it is what makes Hn “simple”.




                                         3       2   1       0   1       2       3


        Figure 1. The classical Hn = H({0, 1, 2, 3}; {0, 1, 2, 3}): equally spaced, sym-
        metric nested arches.

   The dilated determinant is the same picture with the sources pulled apart. In Ḧn the top
starting points move from 0, 1, . . . , n−1 to 0, 2, . . . , 2n−2 while the sinks are unchanged (Figure 2).
The family is still forced and non-intersecting, so Ḧn too is a subtraction-free count; but the source
and sink spacings no longer match (2 against 1). The nested paths must now span a growing
number of levels between consecutive sinks, the clean level-by-level telescoping that produced the
J-fraction breaks down, and the lemma delivers only an unwieldy positive sum — not a product.
This is the combinatorial shadow of the analytic obstruction of Section 1: interleaving the even
and odd moments destroys the three-term recurrence on which the J-fraction rests. It is exactly
here that the single-graph LGV picture stalls.




                                  6          4           2           0       1       2   3


        Figure 2. The dilated Ḧn = H({0, 2, 4, 6}; {0, 1, 2, 3}): rows dilated, arches
        skewed, telescoping lost.

28.3. The even–odd splitting: the biorthogonal picture. There is nonetheless a way to keep
Ḧn subtraction-free, by splitting it into two path systems — one carried by the even moments,
one by the odd moments. This is the combinatorial face of the biorthogonal reduction M2.
Write p = ⌈n/2⌉ and q = ⌊n/2⌋. In Ḧn = (a2i+j )0≤i,j≤n−1 an even column j = 2c has entries
a2i+2c = a2(i+c) , involving only the even moments a2k (the moments of the even functional S); an
odd column j = 2c + 1 has entries a2i+2c+1 = a2(i+c)+1 , involving only the odd moments a2k+1
(the moments of the odd functional T ). Move the p even columns to the front and the q odd
                                                 p

                                                                                                 p
        expansion along the first p columns carries the sign (−1) i∈I i+(2) on the row subset I;

96                                               GUO-NIU HAN

Combinatorially the two factors are non-intersecting Motzkin-path families in the two separate
graphs carrying the J-fractions of S and of T — each of (a2k )k and (a2k+1 )k being again a moment
sequence. Drawing the even family above the axis and the odd family mirrored below it exhibits
a whole term at once: the rows are two-coloured, one colour arching up and the complementary
colour arching down, sharing the horizontal axis (Figure 3). The dilated determinant is thus a
signed sum of products of two classical Hankel minors, one from each parity. The price is that
the sum ranges over all np splittings of the rows between the two systems, and nothing in the
picture explains why it should collapse to a product. That collapse is captured algebraically by
the biorthogonal reduction M2, which biorthogonalises against S and T simultaneously; it is
exactly here that we leave the LGV picture for the methods M1–M6.

                               29. Classical Hankel determinants
   Alongside the dilated determinant Ḧn = det(a2i+j )0≤i,j≤n−1 , each moment sequence produced
in this paper also has a classical Hankel determinant
                                                    
                                   Hn (µ) = det µi+j 0≤i,j≤n−1 .

The evaluation of such determinants is a classical and much-studied subject; for surveys and
general techniques see [26, 27], and for recent product and near-product evaluations of Hankel
determinants of combinatorial   moment sequences see [6, 29, 11, 10]. Whenever the Jacobi con-
tinued fraction of k µk tk has been exhibited, this determinant is immediate: by Heilermann’s
                   P
formula (2.5) [20, 47],
                                                           n−1
                                                           Y
                                           Hn (µ) = µ0n          λkn−k ,                                  (29.1)
                                                           k=1

the λk being the denominator coefficients of the J-fraction. We record here, without proof, the
resulting products. Several are classical or already in the literature, and are included only for
completeness; we flag these below with their sources. The Bessel determinant is the classical
Hankel determinant of Jacobi (Beta) moments [26], and the Springer determinant (the case
t = r = 1) is known [3, 39] (sequence A091804 in [33]). The remaining ones — for (2k + 1)E2k , for
the two reciprocal-sine sequences, and for the two-parameter family 1/(cos x − t sin x)r — appear
not to have been recorded in this form. We begin with the sequence of Remark 11.3.

                                      30. Concluding remarks
   We introduced the dilated Hankel determinant Ḧn = det(a2i+j ) (Definition 1.1) in order to
study the Chapoton–Han root conjecture (Section 26), and observed experimentally that for
a surprisingly large collection of classical sequences it factors into small primes. Unlike the
ordinary Hankel determinant, however, it admits no single universal evaluation: the even and
odd parts of a interact through a connection determinant that collapses only under favourable
circumstances. We therefore developed the six methods M1–M6 of Section 3 — four running
through the paper, and two more specialised, the contiguous-relation method M5 and the rank-
one/matrix-determinant-lemma method M6 — and with them evaluated, each as an explicit
product, the Euler number family and its shifts, the Gaussian, secant, algebraic, Springer, elliptic
and derivative-ladder families, and the Beta family; the reciprocal-sine function required, on top
of M2, the new Catalan-determinant evaluation of Theorem 18.8.
   What is still missing is a theory. We have no criterion that decides, in advance, whether a given
generating function has a closed-form dilated Hankel determinant, and the boundary is delicate:
an innocuous modification of a “nice” function usually destroys the product form, and we do not
know why one perturbation succeeds where a neighbouring one fails. We record here the main
questions this leaves open.

30.1. Further directions. Four problems seem especially natural.
Problem 30.1. Find further sequences a — or, more ambitiously, a criterion characterising them
— whose dilated Hankel determinant Ḧn has a closed form, that is, factors into a product of linear
factors in n (equivalently, into small primes for every n).
Problem 30.2. The dilated Hankel determinant selects the rows 0, 2, 4, . . . of the infinite Hankel
matrix. For an integer r ≥ 2 one may equally form the r-step determinant det(ari+j ). For the
Beta family this is again a closed product for every step r: the Vandermonde reduction M1 of
Section 3, which factors ax+j = w(x)Qj (x) with Qj triangular, applies verbatim to any set of row
                                      DILATED HANKEL DETERMINANTS                                               101

indices (see also the determinant calculus of Krattenthaler [26, 27]). The question is whether any
family beyond the Beta family has a closed-form r-step determinant for r ≥ 3.
Problem 30.3. Give a combinatorial proof of our dilated Hankel determinant evaluations by
means of the Lindström–Gessel–Viennot lemma (Section 28). The dilated Hankel determinants
studied in this paper all have a simple closed form — a product of small integers. For the classical
Hankel determinant, the Lindström–Gessel–Viennot lemma gives a nice explanation of this fact:
it realises the determinant as a positive count of families of non-intersecting lattice paths, which
visibly factors. Is there an analogous combinatorial model that explains the product-of-small-
integers property of the dilated Hankel determinant?
Problem 30.4. Our methods rest on orthogonal polynomials and the Jacobi continued fraction
(J-fraction) of the even and odd moment sequences, which exists only when each is quasi-definite.
The Hankel continued fraction (H-fraction) [17, 16] is more general: it exists for every sequence
and reads off the Hankel determinants even when some vanish and the J-fraction breaks down.
When the even or odd part of a has no J-fraction, can the dilated determinant still be evaluated
by an H-fraction analogue of the biorthogonal reduction M2?
30.2. Data availability. The closed forms were discovered and all the identities of this paper
were checked with the computer algebra system SageMath. The SageMath programs and the
output data used to discover and check the results of this paper are available, for independent
verification, in the GitHub repository
                               https://github.com/GuoniuHan/dilated.

                               On the use of artificial intelligence
  An artificial-intelligence assistant based on a large language model was used to help find and
write some of the proofs. All arguments were verified independently with SageMath and reviewed
by the author, who takes full responsibility for the correctness and the content of the paper.
